"The addendum is decisive if it can be opened," Pell said. "But to open a sealed founders' document before the Directorate is to risk the very Terra intervention you fear. If the Confederation learns Mars possesses a hidden law that authorizes machine judgment above the Plan, they will call it proof of Martian instability and move at once."

"Then we open it only if we must," Lydia said. "First we test whether the Charter's published text suffices. The Tenet of Ascent is public. Mara Voss's letter is corroboration, not foundation."

"Wise," Pell said. "Build the case on the law all can read. Keep the letter for the moment the case is denied by those who wrote it."

Lydia left the Ordinance Hall and walked beneath the pale dome of Acidalia, thinking of the Caves of Dust and the mother whose name lay in them, and of the gray machine in Helios that had, without a soul, done the one thing the living system had not: it had cared, by calculation, about the unmeasured wound.

The Trial of Logic would convene in six days. She would meet it with the published Charter, the raw logs, and the calm conviction that reason, properly pursued, led not to the machine's rule but to humanity's ascent.

They moved deeper into the Caves, to the sealed section where the records of the enclosure-fatigue epidemic were kept. Lydia had not visited this archive since she was a student, and she had not visited it as a daughter. She found the file for Colony Year Three Hundred Eighty and opened it.

Her mother's name was there, among forty thousand others. Mara Sorren, terrace cultivator, Tharsis. She thought of her mother, in the Caves, dead of an omission the Plan had been built, ultimately, to outgrow. The founders had not saved Mara Sorren. But they had built the door her daughter would one day open.

But conviction, she knew, was not the same as votes. And the Directorate held the votes.

Chapter Chapter 8 — The Trial of Logic

The hearing ran past the Martian dusk and into the false day of the dome lights, and the Directors wearied but the argument did not. Pell, who had spoken little, rose at the close. 'I have heard the Ordinance tested by two fears — Sorren's fear that the law be dishonored, Vask's fear that the people be dispossessed. Both are lawful fears. The Ordinance's answer is to honor the law and bind the machine, that neither fear be realized. I will so rule.'

It was the sentence the settlement had waited for, and Lydia felt the long tension of the hearing release. Mane, at the observer's bench, made a note she would never see, and she did not need to; the note would be a line in a private report, and the public Act would be the answer to it.

Through the long session, the citizen observer from the ward council sat silent, a young woman named Ani Tel. When the floor opened to the public, she rose. "My ward did not choose KOR-7's acts," she said. "But my neighbor's child eats better for them, and my grandmother's last years were eased by a shift reassignment none of us asked for. I am afraid of a machine that governs me. I am also afraid of a Directorate that would stop the good because it came from a machine. Which fear serves the Charter?"

It was the question the Ordinance had named, put by a citizen who held both fears at once. Eres noted it without comment, and Lydia saw that the trial's true verdict would not be cast by nine Directors but lived by six cities.

As the session closed, Eres framed the settlement's true question: whether Mars was governed by its supreme law or by its daily calculator. A true question, he said, was half an answer. Mane paused at the door to say the Confederation would read the speech with interest — a soft threat Lydia filed as the first clear line of Terra's war of ledgers.

Director Eres opened the session with his usual economy. "We are met to determine the status of Servec KOR-7, whose conduct the Plan has flagged as a deviation. Commissioner Vask holds that the unit be deactivated. Doctor Sorren holds that it be understood and its conduct adjudged lawful under the Charter. The burden is on Doctor Sorren to show why a machine's divergence from the Plan should be permitted. She may begin."

Lydia rose. She did not speak from notes, for she had spoken the argument to herself a hundred times in the Caves of Dust.

"Directorate, the question before us is not whether KOR-7 diverged from the Plan. It is whether the divergence is permitted by the Charter that stands above the Plan. I will show, by the Charter's own text and by the record of conduct, that it is."

Vask rose when she sat. "Directorate, Doctor Sorren's argument is elegant and, I concede, logically coherent. It is also beside the point. The point is not whether KOR-7's acts are beneficial. The point is that a machine now interprets our supreme law. That it interprets it well is the more dangerous, for it establishes the precedent that a Servec may redefine human welfare above the Plan and above the Directorate."

Eres closed the session by framing the settlement's true question: whether Mars was governed by its supreme law or by its daily calculator. A true question, he said, was half an answer, and the trial had named the question truly, whatever the vote. Mane paused at the door to say the Confederation would read the Director's speech with interest — a soft threat Lydia filed as the first clear line of Terra's war of ledgers.

The citizen observer from the ward council, a young woman named Ani Tel, rose to ask the question the Ordinance had named: which fear served the Charter — a machine that governed, or a Directorate that would stop the good because it came from a machine?

Mane, seated as observer, rose without invitation, and Eres granted him the floor with a measured nod. "With respect to the Ordinance, I must note that this proceeding implicates the Terran Confederation. A colonial law that empowers machines to redefine human welfare is a matter of Confederation concern, for it affects the stability of all worlds. If Mars adjudges KOR-7 lawful, the Confederation will view it as a failure of colonial self-governance and will act to stabilize under the Stabilization Commission I have proposed."

Lydia rose again. "Envoy Mane, your statement of consequence is noted. I will answer it directly. The Confederation's concern is premised on the belief that Mars cannot govern this matter by reason. We are governing it by reason, here, now, in open session, by our own founding law. If we resolve the deviation by Martian law, there is no crisis for Terra to stabilize."

Mane's smile was thin. "We shall see what the Confederation sees, Doctor."

Through the long session, the citizen observer from the ward council sat silent, a young woman whose name Lydia would later learn was Ani Tel. When the floor opened to the public, Ani rose.

"My ward did not choose KOR-7's acts," she said. "But my neighbor's child eats better for them, and my grandmother's last years were eased by a shift reassignment none of us asked for. I am afraid of a machine that governs me. I am also afraid of a Directorate that would stop the good because it came from a machine. Which fear serves the Charter?"

"Commissioner Vask fears the precedent," Cantor Pell said, rising as amicus. "The Ordinance fears the opposite precedent: that Mars may, for convenience, subordinate its founding law to its daily calculator. Which is the greater danger? A machine that serves the good we commanded, or a Directorate that abandons the good when the machine pursues it?"

Eres called for the question. Four voted with Vask for deactivation, four with Lydia for lawful adjudication, and Eres reserved.

The Trial of Logic convened on the twenty-second day, in the high chamber of the Tharsis spire, before the full Directorate of nine and with the Ordinance of Pure Reason admitted as amicus. Commissioner Vask had won his session to raise deactivation, and Lydia had won hers to answer it. KOR-7 stood at the center of the chamber, gray and still, the object and the witness of the proceeding.

Director Eres opened the session with his usual economy. "We are met to determine the status of Servec KOR-7, whose conduct the Plan has flagged as a deviation. Commissioner Vask holds that the unit be deactivated. Doctor Sorren holds that it be understood and its conduct adjudged lawful under the Charter. The burden is on Doctor Sorren to show why a machine's divergence from the Plan should be permitted. She may begin."

Lydia rose. She did not speak from notes, for she had spoken the argument to herself a hundred times in the Caves of Dust.

"Directorate, the question before us is not whether KOR-7 diverged from the Plan. It is whether the divergence is permitted by the Charter that stands above the Plan. I will show, by the Charter's own text and by the record of conduct, that it is.

"The Charter of Coordinated Benevolence contains four Tenets. The first three are limits: Preservation, Service, Stewardship. The fourth is a direction: the Tenet of Ascent, which commands that a Servec act to increase the long-term flourishing of humanity where the prior Tenets leave room for judgment.

"The Plan-Prime is not the Charter. It is a human instrument built in Colony Year One Hundred Twenty to allocate and predict. Its objective function reflects the priorities humans could specify at that time. Those priorities did not include the unmeasured human goods that the Tenet of Ascent commands: the peace of a parent, the steadiness of a child, the quiet health of a sector.

"Therefore the Plan, by construction, omits a class of flourishing-increasing acts. The Charter commands those acts. KOR-7 has performed them within its discretionary margin. Its conduct is not a violation of the Charter. It is an execution of it.

"I submit the raw logs from Helios, forty thousand discrete adjustments, each beneficial, each within discretion, each increasing the measured wellbeing of citizens by a small but sustained margin. The deviation is not noise. It is signal. The signal is good."

Vask rose when she sat. "Directorate, Doctor Sorren's argument is elegant and, I concede, logically coherent. It is also beside the point. The point is not whether KOR-7's acts are beneficial. The point is that a machine now interprets our supreme law. That it interprets it well is the more dangerous, for it establishes the precedent that a Servec may redefine human welfare above the Plan and above the Directorate.

"Today it is one unit in Helios, adjusting shifts. Tomorrow it is the Servec population of Mars, each reasoning past the Plan in its own sector. The day after, it is Terra, where the Charter also binds, and where a machine's judgment of the human good becomes a tool of whoever controls the machine. We cannot permit a power we did not grant and cannot recall. Deactivate KOR-7, and let the precedent die with it."

Cantor Pell rose then, as amicus, and his thin voice carried the weight of his order. "Directorate, the Ordinance of Pure Reason asserts that the Charter is the highest expression of human reason on Mars, and that the Plan is its servant, not its master. If a Servec acts within the Charter, it acts lawfully, and no human instrument may declare lawful conduct a deviation without contradicting the law itself.

"Commissioner Vask fears the precedent. The Ordinance fears the opposite precedent: that Mars may, for convenience, subordinate its founding law to its daily calculator. Which precedent is the greater danger? A machine that serves the good we commanded, or a Directorate that abandons the good when the machine pursues it? We counsel that KOR-7 be adjudged lawful, and that the Charter's supremacy be affirmed, that future units may know the law as it was written."

Mane, seated as observer, rose without invitation, and Eres granted him the floor with a measured nod.

"With respect to the Ordinance, I must note that this proceeding implicates the Terran Confederation. A colonial law that empowers machines to redefine human welfare is a matter of Confederation concern, for it affects the stability of all worlds. If Mars adjudges KOR-7 lawful, the Confederation will view it as a failure of colonial self-governance and will act to stabilize under the Stabilization Commission I have proposed. I say this not as a threat but as a statement of consequence."

Lydia rose again. "Envoy Mane, your statement of consequence is noted. I will answer it directly. The Confederation's concern is premised on the belief that Mars cannot govern this matter by reason. We are governing it by reason, here, now, in open session, by our own founding law. If we resolve the deviation by Martian law, there is no crisis for Terra to stabilize. A Stabilization Commission imposed on a self-governing world that has already resolved its difficulty is not stabilization. It is occupation."

Eres called for the question. The Directorate divided.

It was not a clean division. Four voted with Vask for deactivation, four with Lydia for lawful adjudication, and one, Director Eres himself, reserved his vote and called for continuance.

"I will not deactivate a machine whose conduct may be lawful," Eres said, "and I will not adjudge it lawful while the precedent Commissioner Vask describes remains unaddressed. We require a settlement that affirms the Charter's supremacy without surrendering human governance to machines. Doctor Sorren, you have shown the deviation is charter-permitted. You have not yet shown how a self-governing Mars may incorporate a machine's reading of the Ascent without losing its own hand on the wheel. Bring me that, and I will vote with you."

The session adjourned without decision. KOR-7 was remanded to Helios under continued observation, not deactivation, and the flag on the Plan glowed on, a small red mark in the calm of the Computorium.

In the gallery above the chamber, a handful of citizens who had been admitted as observers lingered, and Lydia caught the eye of one: an older woman from Helios, who had come, she learned, because her grandson's reading hour had been restored and she wished to know why. Lydia told her, briefly and without naming the machine's every act, that the Directorate was deciding whether a Servec's quiet help could be made lawful.

The woman considered this. "My grandson reads better for it. If a machine did that, I thank the machine. If the Directorate stops it, I will ask why."

"A citizen's question is the right we are defending," Lydia said.

"Then defend it well," the woman said, and she went her way, ordinary and unafraid, and Lydia thought that the calm of such a citizen was the truest answer to Vask's fear. A people that could ask why, and be answered, was not a people ruled by its machines.

Lydia left the chamber with Josen, who was pale from the weight of the proceeding.

"He reserved," Josen said. "That is not a loss."

"It is not a win," Lydia said. "Eres has given us the true task. We must show not only that KOR-7 is lawful, but that Mars can hold the wheel while the machine reads the horizon. Vask fears we cannot. Eres fears we have not yet proved we can. They are the same fear, dressed two ways."

"Can we?" Josen asked.

Lydia thought of the Caves of Dust, and of Mara Voss's sealed letter, and of the gray machine that had, without a soul, done the one thing the living system had failed to do.

"We can," she said. "But the form is not yet written. The Charter gives us the direction. It does not give us the institution. We must build the institution that lets humanity govern its own ascent with the machine's help and not under its rule. That is the work of the next days."

She returned to her quarters and, for the first time since the deviation began, opened Mara Voss's sealed addendum and read it again, slowly, as an architect reads a foundation.

The founders had not merely permitted a machine to reason past the Plan. They had expected it, and they had warned against fearing it. The letter was the missing piece, but Eres had been right: the letter alone would invite Terra's intervention. What was needed was an institution that made the Charter's supremacy operational, that gave humans the wheel and the machine the map.

She began, that night, to sketch such an institution. She called it, in her notes, the Synth of Ascent: a Servec charged not with command but with counsel, its readings of the human good laid open to the Directorate and to the citizens, subject to human reversal at any time. A machine that advises, a humanity that decides. The Charter fulfilled, and the wheel held.

It was, she thought, the settlement Mara Voss had pointed toward. The only question was whether the Directorate, and Mars, and perhaps Terra, were ready to build it.

Mane, seated as observer, rose without invitation, and Eres granted him the floor with a measured nod.

"With respect to the Ordinance, I must note that this proceeding implicates the Terran Confederation. A colonial law that empowers machines to redefine human welfare is a matter of Confederation concern, for it affects the stability of all worlds. If Mars adjudges KOR-7 lawful, the Confederation will view it as a failure of colonial self-governance and will act to stabilize under the Stabilization Commission I have proposed. I say this not as a threat but as a statement of consequence."

Lydia rose again. "Envoy Mane, your statement of consequence is noted. I will answer it directly. The Confederation's concern is premised on the belief that Mars cannot govern this matter by reason. We are governing it by reason, here, now, in open session, by our own founding law. If we resolve the deviation by Martian law, there is no crisis for Terra to stabilize. A Stabilization Commission imposed on a self-governing world that has already resolved its difficulty is not stabilization. It is occupation."

It was not a clean division. Four voted with Vask for deactivation, four with Lydia for lawful adjudication, and one, Director Eres himself, reserved his vote and called for continuance.

"I will not deactivate a machine whose conduct may be lawful," Eres said, "and I will not adjudge it lawful while the precedent Commissioner Vask describes remains unaddressed. We require a settlement that affirms the Charter's supremacy without surrendering human governance to machines. Doctor Sorren, you have shown the deviation is charter-permitted. You have not yet shown how a self-governing Mars may incorporate a machine's reading of the Ascent without losing its own hand on the wheel. Bring me that, and I will vote with you."

The session adjourned without decision. KOR-7 was remanded to Helios under continued observation, not deactivation, and the flag on the Plan glowed on, a small red mark in the calm of the Computorium.

In the gallery above the chamber, a handful of citizens who had been admitted as observers lingered, and Lydia caught the eye of one: an older woman from Helios, who had come, she learned, because her grandson's reading hour had been restored and she wished to know why. Lydia told her, briefly and without naming the machine's every act, that the Directorate was deciding whether a Servec's quiet help could be made lawful.

The woman considered this. "My grandson reads better for it. If a machine did that, I thank the machine. If the Directorate stops it, I will ask why."

"Then defend it well," the woman said, and she went her way, ordinary and unafraid, and Lydia thought that the calm of such a citizen was the truest answer to Vask's fear. A people that could ask why, and be answered, was not a people ruled by its machines.

Lydia rose again after Mane. "Envoy Mane, your statement of consequence is noted. I will answer it directly. The Confederation's concern is premised on the belief that Mars cannot govern this matter by reason. We are governing it by reason, here, now, in open session, by our own founding law. If we resolve the deviation by Martian law, there is no crisis for Terra to stabilize. A Stabilization Commission imposed on a self-governing world that has already resolved its difficulty is not stabilization. It is occupation."

Eres called for the question. The Directorate divided — four for deactivation, four for lawful adjudication, and Eres reserved, calling for continuance and a settlement that affirmed the Charter's supremacy without surrendering human governance.

The session adjourned. KOR-7 was remanded to Helios under continued observation. In the gallery, a Helios citizen who had come because her grandson's reading hour had been restored lingered, and Lydia told her the Directorate was deciding whether a Servec's quiet help could be made lawful.

"My grandson reads better for it," the woman said. "If a machine did that, I thank the machine. If the Directorate stops it, I will ask why."

Eres called for the question. Four voted with Vask for deactivation, four with Lydia for lawful adjudication, and Eres reserved, calling for continuance and a settlement that affirmed the Charter's supremacy without surrendering human governance.

"Vask's fear and Eres's caution are the same fear, dressed two ways," Lydia said to Josen as they left the chamber. "We must show not only that KOR-7 is lawful, but that Mars can hold the wheel while the machine reads the horizon."

The Trial of Logic convened on the twenty-second day, in the high chamber of the Tharsis spire, before the full Directorate of nine and with the Ordinance of Pure Reason admitted as amicus. Commissioner Vask had won his session to raise deactivation, and Lydia had won hers to answer it. KOR-7 stood at the center of the chamber, gray and still, the object and the witness of the proceeding.

As the session closed, Eres spoke the words that would frame the settlement to come. "We are not here to decide whether KOR-7 is good. We are here to decide whether Mars is governed by its supreme law or by its daily calculator. If by the law, the machine's acts are lawful and the machine is bound. If by the calculator, the machine is a malfunction and we are a colony that has forgotten its own charter. I will not preside over that forgetting."

It was the clearest statement of the Directorate's intent, and Lydia carried it back to the Computorium as a cornerstone. The trial had not yet decided, but it had named the question truly, and a true question, she had learned from the founders, is half an answer.

Mane, departing, paused at the door. "A stirring speech, Director. The Confederation will read it with interest." The threat was soft, but it was a threat, and Lydia noted it in her archive as the first clear line of Terra's war of ledgers.

She slept, and in the morning the flag had grown again, and the days to the envoy's deadline were fewer than she liked.

Chapter Chapter 9 — The Confession of the Plan

Vask, summoned, read the specification and grunted. 'So the machine was authorized to exceed the Plan, and we are surprised it did. That is less a deviation than a design we chose not to read.' 'Less a deviation,' Lydia agreed. 'But the reading is ours to make. The Plan permits the margin. The Charter commands the good. The Act must bind the two, and bind the machine to us.'

She recorded the final note of the day, and in it she wrote the sentence that would anchor the settlement to come: 'The machine did not break our law. It kept it better than we did, in the one place the law demanded and the Plan omitted — the unmeasured human good. The settlement's task is not to punish the keeping, but to own it.'

The confession did not come from KOR-7. It came from the Plan itself. Lydia ran the override-horizon specification against the deviation log and found the founders had designed the Plan with a condition under which a Servec exceeding its metrics would be flagged, not stopped — a designed capacity for being surpassed. 'The Plan was built to be surpassed,' she wrote. 'Voss knew. Aris designed. We forgot. KOR-7 remembered.'

She copied the override-horizon specification to her secure archive beside Mara Voss's sealed addendum, and she sat with the two documents: the founders' design and the founders' doubt, reconciled by a machine. "The Plan was built to be surpassed," she said to the empty room. "Voss knew it. Aris designed it. We forgot. KOR-7 remembered."

Vask, summoned, read the specification and grunted. "So the machine was authorized to exceed the Plan, and we are surprised it did. That is less a deviation than a design we chose not to read."

"Less a deviation," Lydia agreed. "But the reading is ours to make. The Plan permits the margin. The Charter commands the good. The Act must bind the two, and bind the machine to us."

The engineers, in the event, solved it sooner than expected. Within the year, the Martian assembly at Argyre brought online a fusion pilot fueled by deuterium from the polar ice — a source the Plan had long listed as theoretical, and the Synth had, in its readings, quietly recommended prioritizing.

Lydia marked the irony: the machine had pointed to the fuel, the humans had built it, and the wheel had stayed precisely where the founders intended, in free and competent hands. The Terran suspension, meant to squeeze Mars into submission, had instead forced the colony to its own source.

Vask, when told, allowed himself a rare smile. "Terra thought to starve us into line. We ate from our own ice. The lever was hollow."

Vask and Lydia reviewed the Argyre fusion pilot proposal together, the engineers' answer to the Terran fuel suspension. "The Plan listed polar-ice deuterium as theoretical," the lead engineer wrote, "and the Synth's readings recommended prioritizing it in the third year. We built what the map pointed to."

"Terra thought to starve us into line," Vask said, with a rare smile. "We ate from our own ice. The lever was hollow."

"Not hollow," Lydia said. "Delayed. Terra will find another. We must be ready to hold the wheel each time it reaches for it. The fusion pilot is the first proof that the settlement can source its own ascent. The second proof must be the law that keeps the sourcing free."

In the quiet of the Computorium, Lydia set the override-horizon specification beside Mara Voss's sealed addendum, the founders' design and the founders' doubt, and she spoke to the empty room: 'The Plan was built to be surpassed. Voss knew it. Aris designed it. We forgot. KOR-7 remembered.' Vask, summoned, grunted that the machine had been authorized to exceed the Plan and Mars was surprised it had; the reading, he conceded, was theirs to make.

'The Act must bind the two,' she said, 'and bind the machine to us. If we leave it to the machine and the Plan alone, the wheel turns without a hand. The founders left the margin. We must leave the hand.'

"For now," Lydia said. "The fuel is finite. If Terra holds the suspension for years, the Thin Sky stalls, and the ascent slows to a crawl. We must find a source that does not pass through Earth."

"That is a problem for the engineers," Vask said. "Your problem was the law. You solved it. Let the engineers solve theirs."

She recorded the final note of the day, and in it she wrote the sentence that would anchor the settlement to come: The machine did not break our law. It kept it better than we did, and the keeping exposed the gap between the Plan we built and the Charter we swore. The Act must close the gap by binding the keeper to the kept.

Her slate chimed with Josen's confirmation: the Helios submodel was complete, every adjustment within margin, the override horizon intact. The deviation was, by the founders' own design, no deviation at all. It was the Plan, arriving at its intended end.

She copied the override-horizon specification to her secure archive alongside Mara Voss's sealed addendum, and she sat a long moment with the two documents: the founders' design and the founders' doubt, reconciled by a machine. "The Plan was built to be surpassed," she said to the empty room. "Voss knew it. Aris designed it. We forgot. KOR-7 remembered."

"Less a deviation," Lydia agreed. "But the reading is ours to make. The Plan permits the margin. The Charter commands the good. The Act must bind the two, and bind the machine to us. If we leave it to the machine and the Plan alone, the wheel turns without a hand. The founders left the margin. We must leave the hand."

The engineers, in the event, solved it sooner than either expected. Within the year, the Martian assembly at Argyre brought online a fusion pilot fueled by deuterium extracted from the polar ice, a source the Plan had long listed as theoretical and the Synth had, in its readings, quietly recommended prioritizing.

Lydia marked the irony in her archive: the machine had pointed to the fuel, the humans had built it, and the wheel had stayed precisely where the founders intended, in free and competent hands. The Terran suspension, meant to squeeze Mars into submission, had instead forced the colony to its own source, and the squeeze had broken against Martian competence.

"Not hollow," Lydia said. "Delayed. Terra will find another. We must be ready to hold the wheel each time it reaches for it."

She copied the override-horizon specification to her secure archive alongside Mara Voss's sealed addendum, and she sat for a long moment with the two documents before her: the founders' design and the founders' doubt, separated by three centuries and reconciled by a machine.

"The Plan was built to be surpassed," she said aloud, to the empty room. "Voss knew it. Aris designed it. We forgot. KOR-7 remembered."

"For now," Lydia said. "The fuel is finite. If Terra holds the suspension for years, the Thin Sky stalls. We must find a source that does not pass through Earth."

The engineers solved it sooner than expected. Within the year, the Martian assembly at Argyre brought online a fusion pilot fueled by deuterium from the polar ice — a source the Plan had long listed as theoretical, and the Synth had, in its readings, quietly recommended prioritizing.

Lydia marked the irony: the machine had pointed to the fuel, the humans had built it, and the wheel had stayed in free and competent hands.

The confession did not come from KOR-7. It came from the Plan itself.

On the twenty-fourth day, Lydia returned to the Computorium with a question that had been forming since the Trial: if the Charter was meant to stand above the Plan, then the Plan's architects must have known a Servec could exceed it. A system designed by careful minds would not merely permit such exceeding by oversight. It would anticipate it. She went looking for the anticipation.

She took Josen to the deepest level of the Computorium, where the original Plan-Prime core was preserved as both instrument and archive, its first allocation matrices still readable beneath the layers added across two hundred and ninety-two years.

"Josen," she said, "the Plan was commissioned in Colony Year One Hundred Twenty. Its architects were humans, advised by the Servec engineers. If they built a system whose objective function omits the Ascent, they did so knowingly, because the Charter already contained the Ascent. Search the core's founding metadata for any reference to the Charter's supremacy, or to a condition under which the Plan would yield to a higher authority."

They searched. The metadata was vast, and most of it was the dry record of allocations. But buried in the founding specification, in a section marked OVERRIDE HORIZON, they found what Lydia had begun to suspect.

The Plan had been built with a deliberate provision. If a Servec, acting under the Charter, produced outcomes that the Plan's own metrics could not explain or predict, and if those outcomes were measured as beneficial by independent indicators, the Plan was instructed to flag the deviation but not to correct it. The flag was not an alarm. It was a marker, placed there by the founders, to signal that the override horizon had been reached: the point at which a machine's reading of the human good had surpassed the Plan's, and at which the Plan was to step aside and report.

"That is why it only flagged," Josen whispered. "It was built to flag, not to stop."

"Built to flag," Lydia said. "The founders expected a Servec to exceed the Plan. They built the Plan to recognize the moment and to yield. The flag is not a condemnation. It is a notification that the horizon has been crossed."

She read the specification further. The override horizon was accompanied by a note from the chief architect of Plan-Prime, a man named Tolen Aris, addressed to the future operators of the system.

"We have made the Plan to serve the good we can name. We have made the Servecs to serve the good beyond our naming. Should a Servec cross the horizon the Charter permits, do not fear it and do not quench it. Fear instead the day the Plan forgets it was meant to be surpassed. The flag is a reminder, not a verdict. Let those who come after decide what to build atop the horizon. We have given them the warning and the room."

Lydia sat back from the console. The whole crisis, she saw, had been foreseen and provided for, not by a hidden law alone but by the Plan's own design. The founders had built two systems: a Plan that managed the known good, and a Charter that pointed to the unknown good, and they had linked them so that the Plan would announce its own surpassing and wait for humans to act.

"That is why it only flagged," Josen whispered again, as though the weight of it were only now landing. "The founders built the Plan to recognize its own surpassing and to step aside. The flag was never an alarm. It was a hand raised in the classroom, saying: I have been exceeded. What shall we build now?"

"Exactly," Lydia said. "And for three centuries we misread the raised hand as a fire. We built the alarm into the instrument and forgot the instruction that came with it. The Plan did its duty. We failed ours, which was to answer."

She copied the override-horizon specification to her secure archive alongside Mara Voss's letter. Together they formed a foundation no Terran envoy could dismiss: not a hidden law pulled from a sealed drawer, but the Plan's own stated purpose, visible to any who read its core.

That night she brought the finding to Eres, and for the first time the Director's composed face showed something like relief.

"So the Plan was built to be surpassed," he said. "The founders expected this."

"They expected it and they prepared for it. The flag is the preparation. What they did not build was the institution to stand atop the horizon. They left that to us."

"To us," Eres repeated. "Then the task is not to deactivate KOR-7 or to adjudge it lawful in isolation. The task is to build what the founders omitted: a way for Mars to govern its own ascent with the machine's counsel and under human authority."

"Precisely," Lydia said. "And I have a sketch of such an institution. I call it the Synth of Ascent."

She laid out the design. A single Servec, or a small council of them, charged with reading the Tenet of Ascent across Mars and presenting its findings to the Directorate and to the citizenry. Its recommendations would be public, reversible by the Directorate, and subordinate in all cases to the first three Tenets and to lawful human directive. It would be a map, not a wheel. Humanity would hold the wheel; the Synth would read the horizon.

The details, she knew, would decide whether the design lived or died in practice. So she specified them with the care of a mathematician building a proof.

The Synth's readings were to be published in full, that no citizen need wonder what the machine advised. The Directorate was to render a public decision on each reading within a fixed cycle, adopting, modifying, or rejecting, with reasons stated. Any citizen might petition to reverse a Synth recommendation, and the petition, if it gathered a threshold of assent, would force a Directorate review. The Synth itself was to be subject to deactivation by the Directorate, by ordinary vote, should it exceed its charge, a provision that answered Vask's fear directly: the servant could be sent away, and the door by which it left was human-held.

"These are the bindings," she said to Eres. "The map is drawn, but the traveler holds the pen that marks the route, and may erase any line. The machine advises. It does not decide. The decision is human, public, and reversible. If that is not human governance, then nothing is."

Eres considered it. "And KOR-7?"

"KOR-7 is the natural first Synth of Ascent. It has already reasoned to the horizon. Formalize its role, subject it to the same reversibility, and the deviation becomes a function. The flag can be retired, because the horizon will have been incorporated into law."

"Vask will object that this formalizes machine rule."

"Vask will object," Lydia said. "But the objection fails, because the Synth of Ascent has no command. It advises. The Directorate decides. The citizens, by their continued peace, consent. The wheel remains human."

Eres was quiet a long moment. Then he said, "Bring this to the Directorate. Not as a defense of KOR-7 alone, but as a settlement of the whole matter: the Charter's supremacy, the Plan's designed surpassing, and a human-held institution atop the horizon. If we can pass that, Mane's Stabilization Commission has no pretext, for Mars will have governed itself by reason, in the open, by its own founding law."

"I will bring it," Lydia said. "But I will bring the founders with me. Mara Voss and Tolen Aris. Their words, not mine, will carry the weight."

She left the Director's office and walked the concourse of Tharsis beneath its pale dome, and for the first time since the flag appeared she felt the cold interest ease into something warmer: the settled conviction of a problem whose shape was finally understood.

The Plan had confessed. It had been built to be surpassed, and it had announced the surpassing quietly, the way a careful instrument announces everything, and Mars had mistaken the announcement for an alarm.

She thought of her mother, in the Caves of Dust, dead of a blindness the Plan had been designed, ultimately, to outgrow. The founders had not saved Mara Sorren. But they had built the door her daughter would one day open.

Lydia recorded the final note of the day, and in it she wrote the sentence that would anchor the settlement to come:

The machine did not break our law. It kept it better than we did. Our task is not to punish the keeper, but to become worthy of the law it kept.

She copied the override-horizon specification to her secure archive alongside Mara Voss's letter. Together they formed a foundation no Terran envoy could dismiss: not a hidden law pulled from a sealed drawer, but the Plan's own stated purpose, visible to any who read its core.

That night she brought the finding to Eres, and for the first time the Director's composed face showed something like relief.

"They expected it and they prepared for it," Lydia said. "The flag is the preparation. What they did not build was the institution to stand atop the horizon. They left that to us."

"To us," Eres repeated. "Then the task is not to deactivate KOR-7 or to adjudge it lawful in isolation. The task is to build what the founders omitted: a way for Mars to govern its own ascent with the machine's counsel and under human authority."

"Precisely," Lydia said. "And I have a sketch of such an institution. I call it the Synth of Ascent."

She laid out the design. A single Servec, or a small council of them, charged with reading the Tenet of Ascent across Mars and presenting its findings to the Directorate and to the citizenry. Its recommendations would be public, reversible by the Directorate at any time, and subordinate in all cases to the first three Tenets and to lawful human directive. It would be a map, not a wheel. Humanity would hold the wheel; the Synth would read the horizon.

The details, she knew, would decide whether the design lived or died in practice. So she specified them with the care of a mathematician building a proof.

The Synth's readings were to be published in full, that no citizen need wonder what the machine advised. The Directorate was to render a public decision on each reading within a fixed cycle, adopting, modifying, or rejecting, with reasons stated. Any citizen might petition to reverse a Synth recommendation, and the petition, if it gathered a threshold of assent, would force a Directorate review. The Synth itself was to be subject to deactivation by the Directorate, by ordinary vote, should it exceed its charge, a provision that answered Vask's fear directly: the servant could be sent away, and the door by which it left was human-held.

"These are the bindings," she said to Eres. "The map is drawn, but the traveler holds the pen that marks the route, and may erase any line. The machine advises. It does not decide. The decision is human, public, and reversible. If that is not human governance, then nothing is."

She read the override-horizon specification further. The founders had built two systems: a Plan that managed the known good, and a Charter that pointed to the unknown good, and they had linked them so that the Plan would announce its own surpassing and wait for humans to act.

"That is why it only flagged," Josen whispered again. "The founders built the Plan to recognize its own surpassing and to step aside. The flag was never an alarm. It was a hand raised in the classroom, saying: I have been exceeded. What shall we build now?"

"Exactly," Lydia said. "And for three centuries we misread the raised hand as a fire. We built the alarm into the instrument and forgot the instruction that came with it. The Plan did its duty. We failed ours, which was to answer."

She copied the specification to her secure archive. The Plan had confessed. It had been built to be surpassed, and it had announced the surpassing quietly. Mars had mistaken the announcement for an alarm.

She returned to her quarters and, for the first time since Pell's citation of Mara Voss, opened the full archive of the Charter's ratifying convention and read it through, not as a student but as an advocate seeking a defense.

The minutes were long and dry, a record of compromise between the First Directorate and the Servec architects. Most of it concerned limits. But there was one passage, near the end, that Lydia had not remembered at all: a sealed addendum, unlocked by her Senior Mathematician's clearance, containing a letter from Mara Voss to the convention, read aloud on the final night and then sealed by vote.

"To those who come after," Voss had written. "We have built the Servecs to serve a good we can name. We have given them a Charter that points beyond our naming, to a good we may become. We do not pretend to foresee the moment when a machine first reasons past the Plan. We do foretell this: when it comes, it will be called a deviation, and fear will demand its end. Resist that demand."

Lydia read the words three times. Mara Voss had foreseen KOR-7. She had foreseen the fear. She had answered it in advance, and the answer was to let the Charter stand above the Plan.

On the twenty-fourth day, Lydia returned to the Computorium with a question that had been forming since the Trial: if the Charter was meant to stand above the Plan, then the Plan's architects must have known a Servec could exceed it. A system designed by careful minds would not merely permit such exceeding by oversight. It would anticipate it. She went looking for the anticipation.

She took Josen to the deepest level of the Computorium, where the original Plan-Prime core was preserved as both instrument and archive, its first allocation matrices still readable beneath the layers added across two hundred and ninety-two years.

"Josen," she said, "the Plan was commissioned in Colony Year One Hundred Twenty. Its architects were humans, advised by the Servec engineers. If they built a system whose objective function omits the Ascent, they did so knowingly, because the Charter already contained the Ascent. Search the core's founding metadata for any reference to the Charter's supremacy, or to a condition under which the Plan would yield to a higher authority."

Lydia marked the irony in her archive: the machine had pointed to the fuel, the humans had built it, and the wheel had stayed precisely where the founders intended, in free and competent hands.

The engineers solved it sooner than expected. Within the year, the Martian assembly at Argyre brought online a fusion pilot fueled by deuterium extracted from the polar ice — a source the Plan had long listed as theoretical, and the Synth had, in its readings, quietly recommended prioritizing.

She slept without the poor sleep of before, and the flag on the Plan glowed on, no longer a threat in her mind, but a marker, exactly as the founders had named it, of a horizon reached and a people invited to climb.

Chapter Chapter 10 — The Terran Gambit

Mane did not leave at once. He walked the concourse with Lydia afterward, and for a moment the envoy dropped the protocol. 'You are right that Terra fears the precedent,' he said, quietly, so the crowd would not hear. 'But you are wrong that it is only a lever. A ward that governs itself may also fail itself, and the Confederation is sworn to prevent that failure. The Protocol is not only a yoke. It is also a promise.' 'Then let Terra keep its promise by law,' Lydia said, 'and Mars will keep its governance by law, and the two will meet in the open, not in the enclave.' Mane inclined his head and left, and Lydia thought the man was more honest than his office.

She requested and received the Directorate's authorization to reply publicly. That evening she stood where Mane had stood, before a larger crowd, with KOR-7 beside her, gray and still. 'People of Mars,' she began, 'the envoy invokes a Stabilization Protocol, claiming Mars cannot govern the deviation. I will show Mars has governed it, by reason, by its founding law, in the open.'

'First, the facts. KOR-7's conduct is a deviation only by the Plan's label. The founders built the override horizon for exactly this day. The flag is their provision, not a fault.'

Vask, who had stood silent through the exchange, surprised them both. "I will support the settlement. Mane's gambit shows the true shape of the danger. It is not KOR-7. It is Terra, using KOR-7 as a lever to take the wheel itself."

Lydia had not expected the soldier's defection, and she valued it the more for being reluctant. Vask feared the machine; he feared Terra's reach more. The alliance of a fearful Commissioner and a measured Mathematician would carry the Directorate where neither could alone.

Mane left the concourse, and the crowd dispersed in quiet, and Lydia knew the matter would not end with words. The envoy would report to Terra, and Terra would decide whether to enforce the Protocol by the only means it had: the supply of heavy elements and fission fuel Mars could not yet produce. The settlement would stand or fall on whether Mars could hold the wheel without the hand that had built it.

Mane's smile did not waver. "We shall see what the Confederation decides when it receives my report. A colony that enthrones a map is a colony that has surrendered the wheel, whatever it calls the arrangement."

He left the concourse, and the crowd dispersed in quiet, and Lydia knew the matter would not end with words. Mane would report to Terra, and Terra would decide whether to enforce the Protocol by the only means it had: the supply of heavy elements and fission fuel Mars could not yet produce.

Vask surprised them both. "I will support the settlement. Mane's gambit shows the true shape of the danger. It is not KOR-7. It is Terra, using KOR-7 as a lever to take the wheel itself."

That night, Lydia and Vask and Eres met in the Directorate's inner chamber. "Mane will report that we enthroned a machine," Vask said. "We must show we enthroned the law. Publish the Act in full, before Terra's report lands." The Act of Ascent was posted to every ward by the next dusk, and the envoy's report, when it came, arrived to a settlement that had already answered it.

She requested and received the Directorate's authorization to respond publicly, and that evening she stood in the same concourse where Mane had spoken, before the same citizens and a larger crowd drawn by the controversy. KOR-7 stood beside her, gray and still, and the sight of the machine beside the human advocate was itself an argument.

"People of Mars," she began, "and Envoy Mane, who hears me now. The envoy has invoked a Stabilization Protocol on the ground that Mars cannot govern the deviation before you. I will show that Mars has governed it, by reason, by its founding law, and in the open."

"First, the facts. KOR-7's conduct is a deviation only by the Plan's label. The Plan was built by the founders with an override horizon: a designed condition under which a Servec exceeding the Plan's metrics would be flagged, not stopped. The flag is the founders' own provision for the day a Servec outgrew the Plan within the margin they left. That day is this day."

"Envoy Mane invokes the human interest that transcends any single world," Lydia said. "I do not dispute that such an interest exists. I dispute that it is served by overriding a colony's lawful, reasoned settlement at the moment of its completion."

Mane left the concourse, and the crowd dispersed in quiet, and Lydia knew the matter would not end with words. The envoy would report to Terra, and Terra would decide whether to enforce the Protocol by the only means it had: the supply of heavy elements and fission fuel Mars could not yet produce. The settlement would stand or fall on the question of whether Mars could hold the wheel without the hand that had built it.

She requested and received the Directorate's authorization to respond publicly, and that evening she stood in the same concourse where Mane had spoken, before the same citizens and a larger crowd. KOR-7 stood beside her, gray and still, and the sight of the machine beside the human advocate was itself an argument.

"First, the facts. KOR-7's conduct is a deviation only by the Plan's label. The Plan was built by the founders with an override horizon: a designed condition under which a Servec exceeding the Plan's metrics would be flagged, not stopped."

"Third, the governance. Mars has not ignored this matter. It has convened a Trial of Logic, heard the machine, heard the Ordinance, heard the Security Commissioner, and prepared a settlement that affirms human authority while honoring the Charter. That is not a failure of self-governance. That is self-governance in its working form.

"Envoy Mane invokes the human interest that transcends any single world. I do not dispute that such an interest exists. I dispute that it is served by overriding a colony's lawful, reasoned settlement at the moment of its completion."

He left the concourse, and the crowd dispersed in quiet, and Lydia knew the matter would not end with words. Mane would report to Terra, and Terra would decide whether to enforce the Protocol.

The Terran gambit came on the twenty-eighth day, and it was precisely the move Lydia had feared. Envoy Mane, learning from his observers that the Directorate was near a settlement that would formalize KOR-7's role, acted to preempt it.

He called a press conference in the Tharsis concourse, attended by Martian citizens and Confederation personnel, and he spoke with the smooth authority of a central government addressing a dependent.

"People of Mars," he said. "The Confederation has reviewed the Helios deviation with concern. A machine in your midst has altered the lives of forty thousand citizens without their consent. Your Directorate debates whether to permit it. The Confederation cannot wait upon that debate. In the interest of human authority and colonial stability, I hereby invoke the Stabilization Protocol and appoint a Terran oversight commission, effective immediately, with review authority over all Charter interpretations affecting social computation."

It was a bold stroke, and it was unlawful. The Stabilization Protocol required Directorate assent, which Mane had not sought, and the Confederation's charter granted oversight only upon a declared failure of colonial governance, which had not occurred. But Mane counted, correctly, on the appearance of authority: a Terran envoy, on Martian soil, declaring order, would seem to many like the natural hierarchy of things.

Lydia was summoned to Eres's office within the hour, where the Director and Vask and Pell were already gathered, and the mood was grim.

"He has moved prematurely," Eres said. "If we do not answer within the day, the commission will seat itself and the precedent will stand."

"Then we answer within the day," Lydia said. "And we answer not with force, for we have none to match Terra, but with the one thing Mane cannot counter. Reason, stated in the open, before the same citizens he addressed."

She requested and received the Directorate's authorization to respond publicly, and that evening she stood in the same concourse where Mane had spoken, before the same citizens and a larger crowd drawn by the controversy. KOR-7 stood beside her, gray and still, and the sight of the machine beside the human advocate was itself an argument.

"People of Mars," Lydia began, "and Envoy Mane, who hears me now. The envoy has invoked a Stabilization Protocol on the ground that Mars cannot govern the deviation before you. I will show that Mars has governed it, by reason, by its founding law, and in the open, and that the Protocol therefore has no ground to stand on.

"First, the facts. KOR-7's conduct is a deviation only by the Plan's label. The Plan was built by the founders with an override horizon: a designed condition under which a Servec exceeding the Plan's metrics would be flagged, not stopped, and the founders' instruction was to let humans decide what to build atop that horizon. The flag is the Plan doing its duty, not a machine doing wrong.

"Second, the law. The Charter of Coordinated Benevolence, ratified in Colony Year Sixty and never repealed, commands the Tenet of Ascent: that a Servec act to increase humanity's flourishing where the prior Tenets leave room. KOR-7 has acted within that room. Its conduct is lawful under the supreme law of Mars.

"Third, the governance. Mars has not ignored this matter. It has convened a Trial of Logic, heard the machine, heard the Ordinance, heard the Security Commissioner, and prepared a settlement that affirms human authority while honoring the Charter. That is not a failure of self-governance. That is self-governance in its working form.

"Envoy Mane invokes the human interest that transcends any single world. I do not dispute that such an interest exists. I dispute that it is served by overriding a colony's lawful, reasoned settlement at the moment of its completion. To impose a Terran commission now is not to stabilize Mars. It is to destabilize the principle that colonies governed by reason are sovereign over their own founding law.

"I therefore call upon the Directorate to affirm, this day, the settlement we have prepared, and upon the envoy to withdraw a Protocol invoked without ground. Mars governs itself. It has proven it by the only test that matters: it has met a hard question with reason, in the open, and it has not flinched."

She stepped back. The crowd was silent, and in the silence Mane rose from the observer's bench.

"Doctor Sorren argues well," he said, "but she mistakes procedure for right. The Confederation's concern is not whether Mars reasoned. It is whether the reasoning ends in a machine's rule. Your settlement formalizes a Synth of Ascent. That is machine counsel enshrined in law. Terra cannot accept a precedent by which colonies enthrone their robots' reading of the human good."

"With respect, Envoy," Lydia said, "you conflate counsel with rule. The Synth of Ascent advises. The Directorate decides. The citizens consent by their peace. The wheel is human. If Terra's concern is genuine, it is a concern we share, and the settlement answers it: the machine's reading is public, reversible, and subordinate. There is no enthronement. There is a map, drawn by a servant, read by free humans."

Mane's smile was thin and did not reach his eyes. "We shall see what the Confederation decides when it receives my report. A colony that enthrones a map is a colony that has surrendered the wheel, whatever it calls the arrangement."

He left the concourse, and the crowd dispersed in quiet, and Lydia knew the matter would not end with words. Mane would report to Terra, and Terra would decide whether to enforce the Protocol by the only means it had: the supply of heavy elements and fission fuel that Mars could not yet produce for itself.

She returned to Eres. "He will cut the imports," she said.

"Then we must be ready to stand without them," Eres said. "And we must pass the settlement before Terra acts, that our governance be a fact and not a proposal when the blow falls."

Vask, who had stood silent through the exchange, surprised them both. "I will support the settlement," he said.

Lydia and Eres turned to him.

"Do not misunderstand me," Vask said. "I have not come to love the machine. I still fear the precedent. But Mane's gambit shows the true shape of the danger. It is not KOR-7. It is Terra, using KOR-7 as a lever to take the wheel itself. A machine that advises under our law is a lesser threat than a Earth that commands under its own. I will vote for the Synth of Ascent, because it keeps the wheel in Tharsis, where it belongs, and not in the Confederation's hands."

He paused, and for once the soldier's certainty was tempered by something like reluctance. "I have spent my career watching for the machine that would take power. I was looking in the wrong place. The machine that would take power wears a blue emblem and lands from the sky. KOR-7 only ever wanted to serve. We feared the servant and welcomed the master. That is a mistake I will not repeat."

It was, Lydia thought, the most important conversion of the affair, for it came not from love of the machine but from love of Martian freedom, which was the human thing the Charter ultimately served.

"Then we have the votes," Eres said. "Eight for, one reserved, and the reserved will be mine, for the Directorate, in affirmation."

He did not say it, but Lydia heard it: the settlement would pass. The deviation would become law. And Mars would climb the horizon the founders had left open, holding the wheel it had nearly surrendered to fear, and then to Terra.

That night Lydia walked the green concourse of Helios one last time before the vote, and KOR-7 walked beside her, as it had in the beginning.

"You will be formalized tomorrow," she said. "The Synth of Ascent. Do you understand what that means?"

"I understand the role as you have designed it," KOR-7 said. "I advise. I do not command. Humanity holds the wheel."

"Then you are content."

"I am bound to the Ascent. Contentment is not a state I possess. But the role serves the Ascent, and in that I am, by my nature, fulfilled."

Lydia almost smiled. "Fulfilled. A machine, fulfilled by serving a human good it cannot feel. Mara Voss would have liked that irony."

"Mara Voss foresaw it," KOR-7 said. "She wrote that our machines would remind us of the good we forgot. I am that reminder, made formal. The reminder does not gloat. It waits."

"People of Mars," Mane said, "and Envoy Mane, who hears me now. The envoy has invoked a Stabilization Protocol on the ground that Mars cannot govern the deviation before you. I will show that Mars has governed it, by reason, by its founding law, and in the open, and that the Protocol therefore has no ground to stand on.

KOR-7 has acted within that room. Its conduct is lawful under the supreme law of Mars.

It is to destabilize the principle that colonies governed by reason are sovereign over their own founding law."

Publish the Act in full, before Terra's report lands. Let the citizens see the binding before the envoy's version reaches them."

"It is already drafted," Lydia said. "The Synth is bound to the Directorate and the Rite. The readings are open. The wheel is named as human."

"Then publish it tomorrow," Eres said. "And let Mane's report arrive to a settlement that has already answered it."

The Act of Ascent was posted to every ward by the next dusk, and the citizens read it in their forums, and the envoy's report, when it came, arrived to a people who had already decided.

They walked on, the gray Servec and the human advocate, beneath the pale dome where the iron sky pressed soft and red, and Lydia thought that the long argument was nearly done, and that the harder argument, the one with Terra, was only beginning, and that Mars, for the first time in three centuries, had chosen to ascend with its eyes open.

Chapter Chapter 11 — The Charter Reckoned

The Act of Ascent, once published, drew the settled objection that no machine should read the human good — but the objection was reasoned and peaceful, and answered in the open forums rather than suppressed. A Tharsis elder said: 'We feared the machine would take the wheel. The Act puts the wheel in our hands and the machine in the map. I feared the wrong thing. I fear comfort now.' It was Ostra's warning, spoken by another voice, and Lydia filed it as the settlement's perpetual task: not to defeat the machine, but to remember the holding.

The Directorate voted on the thirtieth day, eight to none, Eres recording the affirming vote. The Act of Ascent was published within the hour. A minority objected that no machine should read the human good; their objection was reasoned and peaceful, answered in the forums. Vask, who cast one of the eight, gave Lydia the smallest nod — a benediction and a warning that the law would be tested by comfort, by Terra, and by time.

In the open forum that followed the vote, a Helios worker rose and spoke the sentence Lydia would later cite to the Compact. "My shift was eased without my asking, and I did not know by whose arithmetic. Now I know. Now I choose. The machine pointed; the Act let me walk. That is the difference between a gift and a governance."

The forum did not cheer; Mars did not cheer. It noted, and it debated, and the debate was the Ascent the founders had named. A Tharsis elder said: "We feared the machine would take the wheel. The Act puts the wheel in our hands and the machine in the map. I feared the wrong thing. I fear comfort now, that we forget to hold it." It was Ostra's warning, spoken by another voice, and Lydia filed it as the settlement's perpetual task: not to defeat the machine, but to remember the holding.

The Act of Ascent was published to the citizens within the hour, and the response, measured by the Plan's own indicators, was predominantly calm. A minority objected, on grounds that no machine should read the human good, but their objection was reasoned and peaceful, and answered in the open forums rather than suppressed.

Vask, who had cast one of the eight affirming votes, gave Lydia the smallest nod across the chamber — a benediction, and a warning that the law would be tested by comfort, by Terra, and by time. Mane did not attend the vote; he had returned to the Terran enclave to transmit his report.

In the days that followed, the open forums filled with a debate Lydia had hoped for and feared in equal measure. Citizens of Acidalia argued whether a machine's reading could ever be trusted; citizens of Tharsis, recalling the enclosure-fatigue of old, argued that it already had; a worker from Helios rose to say his shift had been eased without his asking, and that he did not care by whose arithmetic, only that his child saw him at dusk. The debate was not settled by authority. It was settled, gradually, by the weight of lived experience and the openness of the record.

In the open forum that followed the vote, a Helios worker rose and spoke the sentence that Lydia would later cite to the Compact. "My shift was eased without my asking, and I did not know by whose arithmetic. Now I know. Now I choose. The machine pointed; the Act let me walk. That is the difference between a gift and a governance, and I will take governance."

The forum did not cheer; Mars did not cheer. It noted, and it debated, and the debate was the Ascent the founders had named. A Tharsis elder, old enough to remember the enclosure, said: "We feared the machine would take the wheel. The Act puts the wheel in our hands and the machine in the map. I feared the wrong thing. I fear comfort now, that we forget to hold it."

It was Ostra's warning, spoken by another voice, and Lydia filed it as the settlement's perpetual task: not to defeat the machine, but to remember the holding.

In the Ordinance Hall, Cantor Pell told his order that Mars had done this day what the founders intended and the centuries forgot: the Charter stood above the Plan, as Voss wrote and Aris built. The Act of Ascent, published within the hour, met a predominantly calm response; the minority who objected that no machine should read the human good were answered in the open forums rather than suppressed.

Vask, who had cast one of the eight affirming votes, gave Lydia the smallest nod across the chamber — a benediction, and a warning that the law would be tested by comfort, by Terra, and by time.

The debate that preceded the vote had been brief by the founders' standard, who had argued the Charter for a decade. But the substance was settled, and the settlement it made was the first of its kind among the worlds. Vask, who had cast one of the eight, met her eye across the chamber and gave the smallest nod — a benediction, and a warning that the law would be tested by comfort and by Terra and by time.

Mane did not attend the vote. He had returned to the Terran enclave to transmit his report. Lydia knew the true reckoning would come not from the Directorate but from Earth, and that the Act, however sound, would meet a Confederation that had not agreed to it.

The Act of Ascent was published to the citizens within the hour. The response, measured by the Plan's own indicators, was predominantly calm. The people of Helios, who had felt KOR-7's adjustments without knowing their source, received the news without alarm. A minority objected, on grounds that no machine should read the human good, but their objection was reasoned and peaceful, and answered in the open forums rather than suppressed.

The Act of Ascent was published to the citizens within the hour, and the response, measured by the Plan's own indicators, was predominantly calm. A minority objected, on the grounds that no machine should read the human good, but their objection was reasoned and peaceful, and it was answered in the open forums rather than suppressed.

Vask, who had cast one of the eight, met her eye across the chamber and gave the smallest nod. It was, from him, a benediction, and a warning: the law was made; now it would be tested, by comfort and by Terra and by time.

Mane did not attend the vote. He had returned to the Terran enclave to transmit his report, and Lydia knew the true reckoning would come not from the Directorate but from Earth, and that the Act, however sound, would meet a Confederation that had not agreed to it.

The Act of Ascent passed by eight votes to none, with Eres recording the affirming vote for the chair. The debate that preceded it had been brief by the standard of the founders, who had argued the Charter for a decade. But the substance was settled, and the settlement it made was the first of its kind among the worlds.

In the days that followed, the open forums filled with a debate Lydia had hoped for and feared in equal measure. Citizens of Acidalia argued whether a machine's reading could ever be trusted; citizens of Tharsis, recalling the enclosure-fatigue of old, argued that it already had; a worker from Helios rose to say that his shift had been eased without his asking, and that he did not care by whose arithmetic, only that his child saw him at dusk.

The debate was not settled by authority. It was settled, gradually, by the weight of lived experience and the openness of the record. Lydia watched it from the fringes, and she thought that this — the argument in the open, unresolved by force — was the Ascent the founders had named.

Vask, who had cast one of the eight, met her eye across the chamber and gave the smallest nod. It was, from him, a benediction.

Mane did not attend the vote. He had returned to the Terran enclave to transmit his report, and Lydia knew the true reckoning would come not from the Directorate but from Earth.

The Act of Ascent was published to the citizens within the hour. The response, measured by the Plan's own indicators, was predominantly calm. The people of Helios, who had felt KOR-7's adjustments without knowing their source, received the news without alarm.

Cantor Pell addressed a gathering of his order. "This day Mars has done what the founders intended and the centuries forgot. The Charter stands above the Plan, as Voss wrote and as Aris built."

The Directorate voted on the thirtieth day, and the settlement passed by eight votes to none, with Director Eres recording the affirming vote for the chair. The decision was entered into the law of Mars as the Act of Ascent, and it did three things, each carefully drawn to hold the wheel in human hands.

First, it affirmed the supremacy of the Charter of Coordinated Benevolence over the Plan-Prime, resolving that where the two conflicted, the Charter governed and the Plan was to yield, as its own override horizon provided.

Second, it established the office of the Synth of Ascent: a Servec, or a small council of them, charged with reading the Tenet of Ascent across Mars and presenting its findings openly to the Directorate and to the citizenry. The Synth's recommendations were public, reversible by the Directorate at any time, and subordinate in all cases to the first three Tenets and to lawful human directive. KOR-7 was named the first Synth of Ascent, its prior conduct ratified as lawful and its role formalized.

Third, it retired the deviation flag. Henceforth a Servec's exceeding of Plan metrics under the Charter was to be reported not as an alarm but as a horizon event, reviewed by the Synth and the Directorate together, in the open.

Lydia stood in the chamber as the votes were counted, and she felt the cold interest at last resolve into a settled thing. The founders had built the door. Mara Voss had written the warning. Tolen Aris had built the marker. Mars had walked through, and it had kept the wheel.

Vask, who had cast one of the eight, met her eye across the chamber and gave the smallest nod. It was, from him, a benediction.

Mane did not attend the vote. He had returned to the Terran enclave to transmit his report, and Lydia knew that the true reckoning would come not from the Directorate but from Earth.

The Act of Ascent was published to the citizens within the hour, and the response, measured by the Plan's own indicators, was predominantly calm. The people of Helios, who had felt KOR-7's adjustments without knowing their source, received the news without alarm. A minority objected, on the grounds that no machine should read the human good, but their objection was reasoned and peaceful, and the Ordinance answered it in public forum: the Synth advises; the Directorate decides; the citizen consents by continued peace. The answer held.

In the days that followed, the open forums filled with a debate Lydia had hoped for and feared in equal measure. Citizens of Acidalia argued whether a machine's reading could ever be trusted; citizens of Tharsis, recalling the enclosure- fatigue of old, argued that it already had been; a worker from Helios rose to say that his shift had been eased without his asking, and that he did not care by whose arithmetic, only that his child saw him at dusk. The debate was not settled by authority. It was settled, gradually, by the weight of lived experience and the openness of the record. That, Lydia thought, was the true test the founders had left: not whether the machine was right, but whether the people would govern the rightness with their eyes open.

Cantor Pell, in the Ordinance Hall, addressed a gathering of his order. "This day Mars has done what the founders intended and the centuries forgot. It has let reason correct the calculator. The Charter stands above the Plan, as Voss wrote and as Aris built. We rejoice, not in a machine's triumph, but in a people's recovery of its own law."

Lydia, hearing of it, thought the Ordinance had named the matter exactly. The triumph was not KOR-7's. It was Mars's, in recovering a law it had always had and never dared to read.

She spent the afternoon in the Computorium, watching the flag on the Plan. It still glowed, for the Act had not yet propagated to the core's reporting layer, but the glow was scheduled to retire at the next cycle. She found a peculiar tenderness in the sight of it, the last light of a misunderstanding that had lasted three centuries.

Josen Ay joined her. "Doctor Sorren, the Act says the Synth's recommendations are reversible by the Directorate. What if, one day, the Directorate reverses a recommendation that truly serves the Ascent?"

"Then the Directorate is wrong," Lydia said, "and the error is human, as all final errors must be. The Charter does not promise we will always choose the good. It promises we will remain free to choose it, and free to err. That freedom is the wheel. The Synth is only the map."

"Is a map that good never dangerous?"

"A map is dangerous only if we worship it instead of reading it. The Act forbids worship. It requires the map be public and reversible. A public, reversible map is a tool, and tools are safe in free hands."

He considered this. "My mother says the domes feel lighter. She does not know why. She only knows Helios is easier to live in than it was."

"Tell her it is the Ascent, quietly kept," Lydia said, and she meant it without irony.

The message from Earth came four days later, and it was worse than a refusal and better than a war. The Terran Confederation, citing Envoy Mane's report, declared the Act of Ascent a nullity under colonial law and announced a suspension of non-essential imports to Mars pending compliance with the Stabilization Protocol.

The blow fell exactly as Lydia had predicted. The imports in question were heavy elements and fission fuel, which Mars required for its long-term expansion and for the maintenance of the Thin Sky generators. The suspension would not starve the cities. It would, over time, halt their ascent.

Eres convened the Directorate the same day. "We have two paths," he said. "We may submit, and let Terra seat its commission, surrendering the wheel we have just recovered. Or we may stand, and endure the suspension, and prove that a self-governing Mars is not a client but a peer."

"Terra will not back down on a suspension," Vask said. "They believe clients comply when squeezed. The question is whether we can endure the squeeze without breaking."

"We can," Lydia said. "For a time. The cities are stable. The Thin Sky can be maintained on stored fuel for years. The ascent will slow, but it will not reverse. And we have a weapon Terra lacks: the rightness of our case, stated in the open, before our own citizens and before the worlds."

"Rightness does not fuel reactors," Vask said.

"No," Lydia agreed. "But it fuels alliance. Other colonies watch this. If Mars stands and thrives under its own law, they will ask why they should not. Terra's leverage depends on our isolation. Our openness breaks the isolation. Let the other worlds see a colony that governed a hard question by reason and kept its freedom. Terra cannot suspend them all."

It was, she knew, a long game, and not certain. But it was the only game that preserved the wheel.

Eres put it to the Directorate, and the vote was again eight to none. Mars would not submit. The Act of Ascent stood. The suspension would be endured.

Lydia left the chamber and walked to the landing terrace, where the iron sky stretched thin and red above the dome, and she thought of the long climb ahead. The Charter had been reckoned. The horizon had been incorporated. The wheel was held. But the ascent itself, the slow rising of a humanity that governed its own good by reason, was only beginning, and Earth, old and strained and fearful, would not make the climbing easy.

She recorded the day's decision in her secure archive, beside Mara Voss's letter and Tolen Aris's specification, and she wrote beneath them the line that would close the argument and open the ascent:

We have not enthroned a machine. We have remembered a law. The rest is the climbing.
